=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There is a road extending east and west, and N persons are on the road.
The road extends infinitely long to the east and west from a point called the origin.
The i-th person (1\leq i\leq N) is initially at a position X_i meters east from the origin.
The persons can move along the road to the east or west.
Specifically, they can perform the following movement any number of times.

- Choose one person. If there is no other person at the destination, move the chosen person 1 meter east or west.

They have Q tasks in total, and the i-th task (1\leq i\leq Q) is as follows.

- The T_i-th person arrives at coordinate G_i.

Find the minimum total number of movements required to complete all Q tasks in order.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
X_1 X_2 \ldots X_N
Q
T_1 G_1
T_2 G_2
\vdots
T_Q G_Q

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1\leq N\leq2\times10^5
- 0\leq X_1 < X_2 < \dotsb < X_N \leq10^8
- 1\leq Q\leq2\times10^5
- 1\leq T_i\leq N\ (1\leq i\leq Q)
- 0\leq G_i\leq10^8\ (1\leq i\leq Q)
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

5
10 20 30 40 50
4
3 45
4 20
1 35
2 60

Sample Output 1

239

An optimal sequence of movements for the persons is as follows (the positions of the persons are not necessarily drawn to scale):

For each task, the persons move as follows.

- The 4th person moves 6 steps east, and the 3rd person moves 15 steps east.
- The 2nd person moves 2 steps west, the 3rd person moves 26 steps west, and the 4th person moves 26 steps west.
- The 4th person moves 18 steps east, the 3rd person moves 18 steps east, the 2nd person moves 18 steps east, and the 1st person moves 25 steps east.
- The 5th person moves 13 steps east, the 4th person moves 24 steps east, the 3rd person moves 24 steps east, and the 2nd person moves 24 steps east.

The total number of movements is 21+54+79+85=239.
You cannot complete all tasks with a total movement count of 238 or less, so print 239.

Sample Input 2

8
0 1 2 3 4 5 6 100000000
6
1 100000000
8 0
1 100000000
8 4
1 100000000
5 21006578

Sample Output 2

4294967297

Note that some persons may need to move to the west of the origin or more than 10^8 meters to the east of it.
Also, note that the answer may exceed 2^{32}.

Sample Input 3

12
1558 3536 3755 3881 4042 4657 5062 7558 7721 8330 8542 9845
8
9 1694
7 3296
12 5299
5 5195
5 5871
1 2491
8 1149
8 2996

Sample Output 3

89644

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

```
def find_first_ge(node, l, r, x):
    if mx[node] < x: return N  # no
    if r-l == 1: return l
    push(node)
    mid=(l+r)//2
    if mx[node*2] >= x:
        return find_first_ge(left)
    else:
        return find_first_ge(right)
```

Range assign:
```
def range_assign(node,l,r,ql,qr,val):
    if qr<=l or r<=ql: return
    if ql<=l and r<=qr:
       sum[node]=val*(r-l); mx[node]=val; lazy[node]=val; return
    push; recurse; pull
```


Function `assign_and_sum(node,l,r,ql,qr,val)`:
- If fully covered:
    s = sum[node]
    sum[node] = val*(r-l)
    mx[node] = val
    lazy[node] = val
    return s
- push; recurse left/right; pull; return s_left+s_right.
Then for right cost = length*c - s. For left cost = s - length*c.

Let's design code:

```
import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(1_000_000)

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    X = list(map(int, input().split()))
    a = [X[i] - i for i in range(N)]
    size = 4*N + 5
    seg_sum = [0]*size
    seg_max = [0]*size
    lazy = [None]*size

    def build(node, l, r):
        if r - l == 1:
            v = a[l]
            seg_sum[node] = v
            seg_max[node] = v
        else:
            mid = (l+r)//2
            build(node*2, l, mid)
            build(node*2+1, mid, r)
            seg_sum[node] = seg_sum[node*2] + seg_sum[node*2+1]
            seg_max[node] = seg_max[node*2] if seg_max[node*2] >= seg_max[node*2+1] else seg_max[node*2+1]

    def apply(node, l, r, val):
        seg_sum[node] = val * (r-l)
        seg_max[node] = val
        lazy[node] = val

    def push(node, l, r):
        val = lazy[node]
        if val is not None:
            mid = (l+r)//2
            apply(node*2, l, mid, val)
            apply(node*2+1, mid, r, val)
            lazy[node] = None

    def pull(node):
        seg_sum[node] = seg_sum[node*2] + seg_sum[node*2+1]
        seg_max[node] = seg_max[node*2] if seg_max[node*2] >= seg_max[node*2+1] else seg_max[node*2+1]

    def range_assign_sum(node, l, r, ql, qr, val):
        if qr <= l or r <= ql:
            return 0
        if ql <= l and r <= qr:
            s = seg_sum[node]
            apply(node, l, r, val)
            return s
        push(node, l, r)
        mid = (l+r)//2
        s = 0
        if ql < mid:
            s += range_assign_sum(node*2, l, mid, ql, qr, val)
        if mid < qr:
            s += range_assign_sum(node*2+1, mid, r, ql, qr, val)
        pull(node)
        return s
```
Need be careful in range_assign_sum: if ql/qr partial, we call children only if overlap to avoid unnecessary? We can call both with checks; okay. But if ql<=l and r<=qr handled. If partial, push; then:
```
s = range_assign_sum(left,...)
s += range_assign_sum(right,...)
```
This is okay. To reduce calls, check overlap:
```
if qr <= l or r <= ql: return 0
...
```
At top partial, both children may overlap. Fine.

```
    def point_get(node,l,r,pos):
        if r-l==1:
            return seg_max[node]  # or seg_sum
        push(node,l,r)
        mid=(l+r)//2
        if pos < mid:
            return point_get(node*2,l,mid,pos)
        else:
            return point_get(node*2+1,mid,r,pos)
```
Since leaf seg_sum=value, seg_max=value. Good.

Find_first_ge:
```
    def find_first_ge(node,l,r,x):
        if seg_max[node] < x:
            return N
        if r-l==1:
            return l
        push(node,l,r)
        mid=(l+r)//2
        if seg_max[node*2] >= x:
            return find_first_ge(node*2,l,mid,x)
        else:
            return find_first_ge(node*2+1,mid,r,x)
```

Then process Q:
```
    Q = int(input())
    ans = 0
    for _ in range(Q):
        T,G = map(int,input().split())
        k = T-1
        c = G - k
        cur = point_get(1,0,N,k)
        if cur == c:
            continue
        if cur < c:
            j = find_first_ge(1,0,N,c)
            # j should > k
            R = j - 1  # if j==N, R=N-1
            length = R - k + 1
            s = range_assign_sum(1,0,N,k,R+1,c)
            ans += length * c - s
        else:
            # cur > c
            # last index with a <= c
            j = find_first_ge(1,0,N,c+1)  # first > c
            l = j - 1  # last <= c; if j==0 => -1
            L = l + 1
            # block [L,k]
            length = k - L + 1
            s = range_assign_sum(1,0,N,L,k+1,c)
            ans += s - length * c
```

Could implement iterative segment tree to reduce overhead. Let's consider.

Iterative lazy segment tree with power of two:
- size = 1; while size < N: size <<=1
- data_sum = [0]*(2*size)
- data_max = [0]*(2*size)
- lazy = [None]*size? Actually lazy for internal nodes only; can length 2*size.

Build leaves at size+i = a_i; for extra leaves beyond N, need what? Since array length N, operations only within [0,N). For find_first_ge, extra leaves should be -∞? If we set extra leaves to -INF, max of segments beyond N won't trigger. But range_assign should not assign beyond N. For lower_bound, if no index <N has value≥x, find_first_ge should return N. With extra leaves -INF, max_right can return N if using size? Need handle. For upper_bound via first_ge(c+1)-1, if no index <N >c, first_ge returns N, l=N-1. Good. Extra leaves -INF ensures first_ge won't return beyond N. But if extra leaves -INF and we call max_right, it might return size? Need map to N. We can implement custom.

Iterative lazy segment tree operations:
- `all_apply(k, val)`: data_sum[k]=val*length[k]; data_max[k]=val; if k<size: lazy[k]=val.
- `push(k)`: propagate lazy to children.
- `push_path(k)`: push ancestors of leaf k from top to bottom.
- `pull(k)`: update ancestors after update.

Range assign [l,r):
```
def range_apply(l,r,val):
    if l==r: return
    l += size; r += size
    l0=l; r0=r
    # push ancestors of l and r-1
    for i in range(log,0,-1):
        if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0: push(l0 >> i)
        if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0: push((r0-1) >> i)
    while l<r:
        if l&1: all_apply(l,val); l+=1
        if r&1: r-=1; all_apply(r,val)
        l>>=1; r>>=1
    # pull ancestors
    for i in range(1,log+1):
        if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0: pull(l0 >> i)
        if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0: pull((r0-1) >> i)
```
Need length array for each node to compute sum. `seg_len[k]` maybe. Or compute from node level? Store length for all nodes.

Range sum:
```
def range_sum(l,r):
    if l==r: return 0
    l+=size; r+=size
    push_path(l); push_path(r-1)
    res=0
    while l<r:
       if l&1: res += data_sum[l]; l+=1
       if r&1: r-=1; res += data_sum[r]
       l>>=1; r>>=1
    return res
```
But we want assign_and_sum. Could implement range_apply returning old sum? In iterative, before applying, need sum old for canonical nodes. We can during while, before all_apply, add data_sum[k] to old_sum. But need push ancestors first to ensure data_sum[k] current. Then apply. After applying, pull. This can combine.

```
def range_apply_sum(l,r,val):
    if l==r: return 0
    l += size; r += size
    l0=l; r0=r
    for i in range(log,0,-1):
       if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0: push(l0 >> i)
       if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0: push((r0-1) >> i)
    old=0
    l2=l; r2=r
    while l<r:
       if l&1:
          old += data_sum[l]
          all_apply(l,val)
          l+=1
       if r&1:
          r-=1
          old += data_sum[r]
          all_apply(r,val)
       l>>=1; r>>=1
    for i in range(1,log+1):
       if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0: pull(l0 >> i)
       if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0: pull((r0-1) >> i)
    return old
```

Find first_ge(x):
Need find first index in [0,N) with value >= x. With iterative max_right:
ACL `max_right(l, g)`:
```
def max_right(l, g):
    if l == n: return n
    l += size
    for i in range(log,0,-1): push(l >> i)
    sm = e
    while True:
        while l % 2 == 0:
            l >>= 1
        if not g(op(sm, d[l])):
            while l < size:
                push(l)
                l = 2*l
                if g(op(sm, d[l])):
                    sm = op(sm, d[l])
                    l += 1
            return l - size
        sm = op(sm, d[l])
        l += 1
        if (l & -l) == l:
            break
    return n
```
For max monoid, op=max, e=-INF, g(max)<x. Starting l=0. This returns first index where max >= x. Need handle extra leaves -INF. If no index <N, returns N? Let's see if extra leaves -INF, max_right may return size? Actually if all values <x, it will break and return n (size), not N. We can return min(result, N). But if extra leaves -INF, first index with max>=x won't be beyond N, so if result >= N, return N. Good.
But implementing max_right with push and lazy is complex but manageable.

Alternatively, find_first_ge recursive is simpler and likely okay. Maybe combine with iterative range assign? But point_get and find_first recursive plus range assign iterative? Need push consistency. Could implement all iterative for speed.

Because a is nondecreasing and operations assign intervals to constant c. This is like maintaining a piecewise constant nondecreasing sequence. We could use a balanced BST of blocks (intervals with same a value) and support split/merge, sum. Number of blocks? Each operation assigns an interval to c, merging with adjacent blocks if same value. It can increase blocks by at most 2 (split boundaries). Over Q, O(Q) blocks. We can maintain blocks in a sorted list? Need find first index with a≥c and last≤c. Since a nondecreasing, blocks sorted by value. We could use a treap (randomized BST) keyed by start index, storing block value and sum. Operations:
- split by position
- find block containing index for point value
- find first block with value ≥ c: since blocks sorted by value, can search by block value in treap? But treap keyed by start index; to find first index with value≥c, we can traverse treap using max value in subtree? Similar segment tree. Range assign to interval: split treap at L,R, discard middle, create new block value c, merge. Need also range sum of discarded middle. This is O(log blocks) with treap. But implementing treap in Python maybe more complex but fewer operations? Segment tree easier.

Could use `bisect` on blocks if we maintain arrays of starts, ends, values and use union-find? Since operations assign intervals to c and merge; maybe blocks count O(Q). Need find first value≥c; blocks sorted by value because a nondecreasing, so values of blocks nondecreasing. We can maintain block boundaries in arrays and values. Range assign [L,R] to c: find block containing L and R, split, replace middle blocks with one block. Need find first block with value≥c: since block values sorted, use bisect on block values. But block values array changes with insertions/deletions; Python list insertion/deletion O(number of blocks) could be O(Q^2). Could use `bisect` with list and maybe Q=2e5 too slow if many blocks. Treap better.

Range_apply_sum:
```
def range_apply_sum(l,r,val):
    if l >= r: return 0
    l += size
    r += size
    l0 = l
    r0 = r
    for i in range(log,0,-1):
        if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
            push(l0 >> i)
        if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
            push((r0 - 1) >> i)
    old = 0
    while l < r:
        if l & 1:
            old += sumv[l]
            all_apply(l, val)
            l += 1
        if r & 1:
            r -= 1
            old += sumv[r]
            all_apply(r, val)
        l >>= 1
        r >>= 1
    l = l0
    r = r0
    for i in range(1, log+1):
        if ((l >> i) << i) != l:
            pull(l >> i)
        if ((r >> i) << i) != r:
            pull((r - 1) >> i)
    return old
```
Need check push conditions. Standard ACL:
```
for i in range(log, 0, -1):
  if (((l >> i) << i) != l): push(l >> i)
  if (((r >> i) << i) != r): push((r - 1) >> i)
```
This pushes ancestors that are not fully covered? Let's verify. For range apply, before modifying, need push all ancestors of boundary leaves to ensure data in nodes on paths current. The conditions skip pushing if l is aligned at that level? Actually if l is multiple of 2^i, then the node l>>i is fully covered by the range? Wait standard ACL code:
```
for i in range(log, 0, -1):
  if ((l >> i) << i) != l: push(l >> i)
  if ((r >> i) << i) != r: push((r - 1) >> i)
```
Yes.

`find_first_ge(x)` iterative:
We need first index in [0,N) with maxv >= x. Could use ACL max_right with l=0. But maybe easier recursive find_first_ge on iterative tree? We can implement a function using tree descent with lazy push:
```
def find_first_ge(x):
    if maxv[1] < x:
        return N
    k = 1
    while k < size:
        push(k)
        if maxv[2*k] >= x:
            k = 2*k
        else:
            k = 2*k + 1
    idx = k - size
    return idx if idx < N else N
```
Is this correct? Starting at root, if root max < x return N. At internal node, push lazy to children to ensure maxv children current. Then if left child max >= x, go left; else go right. This finds leftmost leaf in the entire tree with value >= x. Since extra leaves have -INF, it won't return extra if no real. If real exists, returns its index. If idx>=N? Could only if extra leaf has max>=x, impossible with -INF. So return idx (should <N). But wait if maxv[1] includes extra -INF, okay. Need if root max>=x due to extra? no.
This is simpler than max_right and O(logN). It doesn't require sorted. Need push along path to ensure children max current. Good.

The transformed operation:
Given nondecreasing a, task (k,c). If a_k<c:
- Let j = first index >k with a_j≥c. Set a_k..a_{j-1}=c.
This is exactly the L1 projection onto constraint a_k=c? Let's prove with isotonic regression. For right side, if a_k<c, to set a_k=c, all following with a<c must be raised to c; first with a≥c remains. Cost sum(c-a_i). This is minimal because each raised by at least c-a_i? Actually if a_i<c for i<k? Wait a_i≤a_k<c for i≤k, so left side already ≤c, no need change. For i between k and j-1, a_i<c, and after setting a_k=c, nondecreasing requires a_i≥c for i>k? Since a_k=c and a_i≥a_k, yes a_i≥c. Thus each must increase to at least c, cost at least c-a_i. For i≥j, a_i≥c already, can stay. So minimal. Similarly left. This is a direct proof of local operation, no need isotonic. Good.

Now, optimality over sequence: At each task, any feasible configuration must have a_k=c. If a_k<c, then for all i>k with a_i<c, due to nondecreasing, they must be at least c after update? Wait if a_k=c, nondecreasing implies a_{k+1}≥c, a_{k+2}≥c, etc. Thus all indices from k until before first already ≥c must be raised to at least c. Minimal cost is exactly raise them to c. No other changes needed. This is forced. Similarly if a_k>c, all indices before k with a_i>c must be lowered to at most c? Actually if a_k=c, nondecreasing implies a_{k-1}≤c, so all before with a>c must lower to c. Minimal. Thus the update is forced by current task and monotonicity, not just myopic! Wait is it forced? Let's examine: If a_k<c, after task a_k=c. Nondecreasing means for i>k, a_i ≥ a_k = c. Therefore any i with old a_i<c must be increased to at least c. They cannot stay below c. So minimal is to set them exactly c. What about indices i<k? They only need ≤c; old a_i≤a_k<c, so okay. Thus the update is the unique minimal configuration satisfying the task? It is the pointwise minimal changes; any feasible config must have those increases, so cost at least sum(c-a_i) for those i. Achievable by setting exactly c. Thus no choice: the minimal configuration is unique? For i>k with old a_i≥c, they can stay c? Actually if old a_i>c, they can stay >c; if old a_i=c, stay. If old a_i>c, could lower to c and reduce? No cost positive; but maybe lower to c could help future? But current minimal would keep them. However, could a non-minimal configuration lower them to c (cost) and help future? We need consider. The update we choose keeps old a_i≥c unchanged. But a feasible configuration could lower some a_i>c to c, incurring cost now, maybe reduce future cost. Is that ever beneficial? Earlier examples showed extra moves can be postponed; but here lowering a_i>c to c is extra. Could it reduce future? Let's test.

Suppose a=[0,100], task1 k=0,c=50. Our update: a=[50,100]. Alternative: lower a1 to50 as well? a=[50,50]? But nondecreasing and a0=50,a1=50. Cost old: a0 +50, a1 -50 total100 vs our cost50. Future task2 maybe k=1,c=50. Our future cost from [50,100] to set a1=50 is 50, total100. Alternative already [50,50], future cost0, total100. Equal. So extra move can be postponed/tied.
What about lowering a_i>c to c could reduce future cost more than current extra? Since moving a_i from old to c now and maybe to future target later costs at least direct old->future? Triangle inequality: current extra + future = d(old, c)+d(c, future) ≥ d(old, future). If instead keep old now, future cost d(old, future) ≤ extra+future. So not worse to postpone. Thus there exists optimal with minimal current update. This is the triangle inequality argument. So greedy is optimal.

But note our update is not just minimal immediate cost; it is the "closest point" in L1 to previous config among feasible. We can prove by exchange: If an optimal path moves a person before it is necessary, move that movement to after the current task; costs no more by triangle inequality and constraints remain satisfied? Need be careful with order constraints, but likely.

Alternatively, since the update is forced for indices that need to cross c, and optional moves of others can be shifted to later without increasing total, greedy is optimal. Good.

Now, let's see if there is any scenario where moving an optional person earlier could allow current target to move less? No, target's a_k fixed c; if a_k<c, all i>k with a_i<c must be raised to at least c; optional persons with a_i>c don't affect. Moving them earlier cannot reduce required raises. So no.

Thus code solves.

### Let's consider if tasks can require moving a person to coordinate that violates order with current neighbors? The operation always maintains order. Since G_i can be any 0..1e8, and road infinite, always possible by shifting all left/right. Good.

### Let's think about initial positions X_i sorted but not necessarily consecutive; a_i nondecreasing but may have equal values. Equal a_i means consecutive positions? p_i = a_i+i, if a_i=a_{i+1}, p_{i+1}=p_i+1, adjacent. Good. Range assignment to c can create equal a blocks.

### Now, let's inspect iterative segment tree code for correctness with `lazy` sentinel and `push` when `lazy[k]` is None. If `all_apply` sets lazy for internal node, and then later `pull` on that node? In range_apply_sum, after applying to a node, we later pull its ancestors, not the node itself. But if a node has lazy and then we call `pull` on it? Could happen if the node is an ancestor of another applied node? We said selected nodes disjoint, no ancestor. But in the pull loop, we pull ancestors of l0 and r0-1. Could one of those ancestors be a node that was itself selected and has lazy? Let's check. Suppose range [2,4) selected node5. l0=10,r0=12. Pull loop:
i=1: l0>>1=5, condition ((10>>1)<<1)=10 == l0? 10==10 so skip left; r0>>1=6, ((12>>1)<<1)=12 == r0 skip right. i=2: l0>>2=2, ((10>>2)<<2)=8 !=10 pull node2; r0>>2=3, ((12>>2)<<2)=12 ==12 skip. i=3: l0>>3=1, ((10>>3)<<3)=8 !=10 pull root; r0>>3=1, ((12>>3)<<3)=8 !=12 pull root? It may pull root twice? Standard code pulls both if conditions; if same node, pull twice okay. It does not pull node5. Good.
If range [1,5) selected leaf1, leaf4, node5. l0=9,r0=13. Pull loop:
i=1: l0>>1=4, ((9>>1)<<1)=8 !=9 pull node4 (parent of leaf1). r0>>1=6, ((13>>1)<<1)=12 !=13 pull node6 (parent of leaf4). i=2: l0>>2=2, ((9>>2)<<2)=8 !=9 pull node2. r0>>2=3, ((13>>2)<<2)=12 !=13 pull node3. i=3: pull root. It does not pull node5? Wait node5 was selected and applied, its ancestors pulled. But node5's parent node2 pulled, so node5's contribution included. Node5 itself has lazy set, sumv current. Good.
Could pull an ancestor that has lazy from previous operation? Suppose node2 had lazy, and we apply to a subrange inside node2. The push loop before applying pushes node2 and descendants along boundaries, so node2 lazy cleared. Then after apply, pull node2 recomputes from children. Good.

Need ensure `push_path` in point_get and find_first_ge pushes all ancestors, including those with lazy. It uses `push(k)` which propagates to children and clears. If a node has lazy and we only need its max for find_first_ge, we push to ensure children max current. Good.

### Let's write iterative code carefully.

Pseudo:

```
import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    X = list(map(int, input().split()))
    a = [X[i] - i for i in range(N)]

    size = 1
    log = 0
    while size < N:
        size <<= 1
        log += 1

    sumv = [0] * (2*size)
    maxv = [0] * (2*size)
    lazy = [None] * (2*size)
    length = [0] * (2*size)

    NEG_INF = -10**30

    for i in range(size, 2*size):
        length[i] = 1
    for i in range(size-1, 0, -1):
        length[i] = length[i<<1] + length[i<<1|1]

    for i, v in enumerate(a):
        sumv[size+i] = v
        maxv[size+i] = v
    for i in range(N, size):
        maxv[size+i] = NEG_INF
        # sumv already 0
    for i in range(size-1, 0, -1):
        sumv[i] = sumv[i<<1] + sumv[i<<1|1]
        maxv[i] = maxv[i<<1] if maxv[i<<1] >= maxv[i<<1|1] else maxv[i<<1|1]
```
Note: For extra leaves, length=1 but sumv=0. For internal nodes, length includes extra leaves. If all_apply accidentally to node with extra, sum wrong. But no. However, for pull after applying to a node with no extra, length of that node equals number of real positions in that segment? If the node is fully within [0,N), yes its segment length equals number of real positions because no extra. Good.

But what about node2 in N=5 size=8: node2 covers [0,4], length=4, no extra. Node3 covers [4,8], length=4 includes extra leaves 5-7. We never apply to node3 as a whole for ranges within [0,5) because node3 not fully contained. We might apply to node6 [4,6]? Range [4,5) selects leaf4, not node6 because r=13? Let's see range [4,5): l=12,r=13. l even,r odd -> r=12 apply leaf12. It doesn't select node6 [4,6] because not fully contained. Range [4,5) only leaf. Range [4,?] if r=6 (not possible N=5) would select node6. So no extra. Good.

But if N=6, size=8, node6 [4,6] fully real, okay. Node7 [6,8] extra. Range [4,6) selects node6. Good.

Now, functions:

```
    def all_apply(k, val):
        sumv[k] = val * length[k]
        maxv[k] = val
        if k < size:
            lazy[k] = val
```
If k is internal but covers extra? Not applied. If k=0? no.

```
    def push(k):
        v = lazy[k]
        if v is not None:
            all_apply(k<<1, v)
            all_apply(k<<1|1, v)
            lazy[k] = None
```
`k` should be < size. If k=0? no. If k>=size, lazy[k] None; but push not called with leaf. In push_path, p>>i for i>=1, p<2size, p>>1 < size. Good.

```
    def pull(k):
        sumv[k] = sumv[k<<1] + sumv[k<<1|1]
        maxv[k] = maxv[k<<1] if maxv[k<<1] >= maxv[k<<1|1] else maxv[k<<1|1]
```

`range_apply_sum`:
```
    def range_apply_sum(l, r, val):
        if l >= r:
            return 0
        l += size
        r += size
        l0 = l
        r0 = r
        for i in range(log, 0, -1):
            if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
                push(l0 >> i)
            if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
                push((r0 - 1) >> i)
        old = 0
        while l < r:
            if l & 1:
                old += sumv[l]
                all_apply(l, val)
                l += 1
            if r & 1:
                r -= 1
                old += sumv[r]
                all_apply(r, val)
            l >>= 1
            r >>= 1
        l = l0
        r = r0
        for i in range(1, log + 1):
            if ((l >> i) << i) != l:
                pull(l >> i)
            if ((r >> i) << i) != r:
                pull((r - 1) >> i)
        return old
```
Need if l0>>i could be 0? l0>=size>=1, i<=log, l0>>log >=1. r0-1>>i >=1. Good.
Potential issue: In push loop, if l0 and r0 conditions both push same node, push twice. If first push clears lazy, second push sees None. Fine. But if l0>>i == (r0-1)>>i and condition true for both, push twice. okay.
Potential issue: In pull loop, if l and r conditions both pull same node, pull twice. okay.
Potential issue: If range_apply_sum applies to a node that is also in the pull path? We said no. But if l0/r0 conditions pull a node that was selected? Example range [0,4) in size=8 selects node2. l0=8,r0=12. Pull loop:
i=1: l0>>1=4, ((8>>1)<<1)=8 == l0 skip; r0>>1=6, ((12>>1)<<1)=12 == r0 skip.
i=2: l0>>2=2, ((8>>2)<<2)=8 == l0 skip; r0>>2=3, ((12>>2)<<2)=12 == r0 skip.
i=3: l0>>3=1, ((8>>3)<<3)=8 == l0 skip; r0>>3=1, ((12>>3)<<3)=8 !=12 pull root. It does not pull node2. Node2 updated by all_apply. Good.
Range [2,4) selected node5, pull ancestors not node5. Good.
Range [1,2) selected leaf9, pull ancestors including node4, node2, root. Not leaf. Good.

`point_get`:
```
    def point_get(p):
        p += size
        for i in range(log, 0, -1):
            push(p >> i)
        return maxv[p]
```
If log=0, no push. maxv[p] leaf. Good.

`find_first_ge`:
```
    def find_first_ge(x):
        if maxv[1] < x:
            return N
        k = 1
        while k < size:
            push(k)
            if maxv[k<<1] >= x:
                k = k<<1
            else:
                k = k<<1|1
        idx = k - size
        return idx if idx < N else N
```
Need if size=1, while false, k=1, idx=0. Good.
If root max>=x due to real leaf, idx<N. If due to extra? no. But if x<=NEG_INF, extra could qualify; not. To be safe, return idx if idx<N else N. Good.
Potential issue: `push(k)` may change maxv[k<<1] and maxv[k<<1|1], but maxv[k] remains val until pull? push doesn't change maxv[k] (still val), lazy cleared. Children updated. Good.
If k has lazy and we push, then check children. Good.
If left child max>=x, go left. This finds leftmost leaf because we always prefer left if any. Good.

Now, process queries:
```
    Q = int(input())
    ans = 0
    for _ in range(Q):
        T, G = map(int, input().split())
        k = T - 1
        c = G - k
        cur = point_get(k)
        if cur == c:
            continue
        if cur < c:
            j = find_first_ge(c)
            R = j - 1
            old = range_apply_sum(k, R + 1, c)
            ans += (R - k + 1) * c - old
        else:
            j = find_first_ge(c + 1)
            L = j
            old = range_apply_sum(L, k + 1, c)
            ans += old - (k - L + 1) * c
    print(ans)
```
Need if j=N in right, R=N-1, R+1=N. Good.
If j=0 in left, L=0. Good.
Let's verify left formula with j=find_first_ge(c+1). Suppose a=[10,17,17,17,46], c=17, k=3. find_first_ge(18)=4, L=4? Wait earlier for sample after task2? Let's re-evaluate. a=[10,17,17,17,46], c=17, k=3. cur a3=17 equals c, so no operation. Not left. For left cur>c, e.g., a=[10,17,17,17,46], c=17, k=4. cur=46>17. find_first_ge(18)=4, L=4, block [4,4] set to17. Is that correct? Need set a4=17; previous a3=17≤17, okay. Cost 46-17=29. Good. If c=16, k=4. find_first_ge(17)=1? a=[10,17,...], first ≥17 is index1. L=1, block [1,4] set to16. But wait a0=10≤16, a1..a4=17,17,17,46 >16, set to16. Nondecreasing: 10≤16≤16. Cost (17-16)*3+(46-16)=31. Could there be a cheaper feasible config? Need a4=16; nondecreasing implies a3≤16, a2≤16, a1≤16. Since a1=17>16, must lower a1..a4 to ≤16. Minimal set all to16. Yes. Good.
If c=16, k=1 (cur=17>16). find_first_ge(17)=1, L=1, block [1,1] set16. a0=10≤16, a2=17≥16. Good.

Right formula example a=[10,17,17,17,46], c=20, k=0 cur=10<20. find_first_ge(20)=4, R=3, block [0,3] set20. Need a1..a3=17<20, raise to20; a4=46≥20. Cost 4*20-(10+17+17+17)=80-61=19. Feasible? a0=20, a1=20,a2=20,a3=20,a4=46. Yes. If c=17, k=0 cur=10<17. find_first_ge(17)=1, R=0, block [0,0] set17. a1=17≥17. Cost7. Good.
If c=18, k=3 cur=17<18. find_first_ge(18)=4, R=3, block [3,3] set18. a4=46≥18. Cost1. a=[10,17,17,18,46]. Good.

### Let's consider if `range_apply_sum` old sum for a selected node with lazy and not pushed? We push ancestors of boundaries, but selected internal nodes not on boundary may have lazy. Is `sumv[k]` current? Yes, if a node has lazy, sumv[k] was set when lazy applied. If its ancestors have lazy, push loop pushes ancestors, which propagates lazy to children including this node? Wait if an ancestor has lazy, and the selected node is inside that ancestor, the push loop should push that ancestor before decomposition. Does the push loop push all ancestors of boundary leaves, not necessarily all ancestors of selected nodes? Standard lazy segtree range_apply pushes ancestors of l and r-1 before applying. Is that sufficient to ensure all selected nodes have correct values? Let's recall ACL lazy segtree: before applying, it pushes ancestors of l and r-1. This ensures that all nodes on paths to boundaries are correct. But what about a selected node deep inside that is not on boundary path? If an ancestor of that selected node has lazy, then that ancestor is also an ancestor of either l or r-1? Since the selected node is within [l,r), its ancestor covers a range that includes l or r-1? Not necessarily? Let's think. If the entire range [l,r) is inside a node with lazy, then l and r-1 are within that node, so the node is an ancestor of both boundary leaves, and push loop will push it. If a selected node is fully covered and its parent has lazy, then the parent's range contains the selected node and also contains l or r-1? Since the selected node is part of the canonical cover; if parent had lazy, the algorithm would not descend into parent? Actually if parent is fully covered by [l,r), it would be selected instead of child, so no child selected. If parent is partially covered, then one boundary lies inside parent, so parent is pushed. Thus selected nodes' ancestors with lazy are pushed. Good. So sumv current.

```
    def push(k):
        v = lazy[k]
        if v is not None:
            lc = k << 1
            rc = lc | 1
            sumv[lc] = v * length[lc]
            maxv[lc] = v
            if lc < size:
                lazy[lc] = v
            sumv[rc] = v * length[rc]
            maxv[rc] = v
            if rc < size:
                lazy[rc] = v
            lazy[k] = None
```
`all_apply`:
```
    def all_apply(k, val):
        sumv[k] = val * length[k]
        maxv[k] = val
        if k < size:
            lazy[k] = val
```
In range_apply_sum, call all_apply. Fine.

`pull`:
```
    def pull(k):
        lc = k << 1
        rc = lc | 1
        sumv[k] = sumv[lc] + sumv[rc]
        maxv[k] = maxv[lc] if maxv[lc] >= maxv[rc] else maxv[rc]
```

### Now, let's write final code with iterative segment tree.

Let's draft code fully:

```
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    ptr = 0
    N = data[ptr]; ptr += 1
    X = data[ptr:ptr+N]; ptr += N

    a = [X[i] - i for i in range(N)]

    size = 1
    log = 0
    while size < N:
        size <<= 1
        log += 1

    sumv = [0] * (2 * size)
    maxv = [0] * (2 * size)
    lazy = [None] * (2 * size)
    length = [0] * (2 * size)

    NEG_INF = -10**30

    for i in range(size, 2 * size):
        length[i] = 1
    for i in range(size - 1, 0, -1):
        length[i] = length[i << 1] + length[i << 1 | 1]

    for i, v in enumerate(a):
        sumv[size + i] = v
        maxv[size + i] = v
    for i in range(N, size):
        maxv[size + i] = NEG_INF
    for i in range(size - 1, 0, -1):
        lc = i << 1
        rc = lc | 1
        sumv[i] = sumv[lc] + sumv[rc]
        maxv[i] = maxv[lc] if maxv[lc] >= maxv[rc] else maxv[rc]

    def all_apply(k, val):
        sumv[k] = val * length[k]
        maxv[k] = val
        if k < size:
            lazy[k] = val

    def push(k):
        v = lazy[k]
        if v is not None:
            lc = k << 1
            rc = lc | 1
            sumv[lc] = v * length[lc]
            maxv[lc] = v
            if lc < size:
                lazy[lc] = v
            sumv[rc] = v * length[rc]
            maxv[rc] = v
            if rc < size:
                lazy[rc] = v
            lazy[k] = None

    def pull(k):
        lc = k << 1
        rc = lc | 1
        sumv[k] = sumv[lc] + sumv[rc]
        maxv[k] = maxv[lc] if maxv[lc] >= maxv[rc] else maxv[rc]

    def range_apply_sum(l, r, val):
        l += size
        r += size
        l0 = l
        r0 = r
        for i in range(log, 0, -1):
            if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
                push(l0 >> i)
            if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
                push((r0 - 1) >> i)
        old = 0
        while l < r:
            if l & 1:
                old += sumv[l]
                all_apply(l, val)
                l += 1
            if r & 1:
                r -= 1
                old += sumv[r]
                all_apply(r, val)
            l >>= 1
            r >>= 1
        l = l0
        r = r0
        for i in range(1, log + 1):
            if ((l >> i) << i) != l:
                pull(l >> i)
            if ((r >> i) << i) != r:
                pull((r - 1) >> i)
        return old

    def point_get(p):
        p += size
        for i in range(log, 0, -1):
            push(p >> i)
        return maxv[p]

    def find_first_ge(x):
        if maxv[1] < x:
            return N
        k = 1
        while k < size:
            push(k)
            lc = k << 1
            if maxv[lc] >= x:
                k = lc
            else:
                k = lc | 1
        idx = k - size
        return idx if idx < N else N

    Q = data[ptr]; ptr += 1
    ans = 0
    for _ in range(Q):
        T = data[ptr]; G = data[ptr+1]; ptr += 2
        k = T - 1
        c = G - k
        cur = point_get(k)
        if cur == c:
            continue
        if cur < c:
            j = find_first_ge(c)
            R = j - 1
            old = range_apply_sum(k, R + 1, c)
            ans += (R - k + 1) * c - old
        else:
            j = find_first_ge(c + 1)
            L = j
            old = range_apply_sum(L, k + 1, c)
            ans += old - (k - L + 1) * c

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

### Now, let's think about if there is a simpler proof that the update is forced:
At task, a_k=c. Since a nondecreasing:
- If old a_k<c, then for all i>k, need a_i≥c. The first index j with old a_j≥c can stay; all k≤i<j have old a_i<c and must increase to at least c. Minimum increase to c. For i<k, old a_i≤old a_k<c, can stay. So unique minimal update.
- If old a_k>c, symmetric.
This shows the greedy update is the minimal cost configuration satisfying current task. But optimal over sequence? We can argue by induction: There exists an optimal strategy that after each task is at the minimal update from previous. Suppose an optimal strategy after task t is at some configuration y that is not the minimal update x from previous z. The extra movement from z to y can be decomposed; if we instead go from z to x, then from x to the next configuration, the total cost is no larger by triangle inequality? Need compare d(z,y)+d(y,next) vs d(z,x)+d(x,next). Since d(z,y) = d(z,x)+d(x,y) if x lies on a shortest path from z to y? Is x on a shortest path? The minimal update x is coordinatewise between z and y? For right move, z has a_i<c for block, x sets them to c, y (feasible) must have a_i≥c for those i. Thus y_i ≥ x_i ≥ z_i for block; for others y_i=z_i maybe or more. So x is between z and y coordinatewise for changed coordinates? For i in block, z_i < c ≤ y_i, so x_i=c between. For i not in block, x_i=z_i, y_i could differ. If y_i differs, extra movement not on path? But if y_i≠z_i for optional persons, then x_i=z_i, so d(z,y) includes |y_i-z_i|, while d(z,x)+d(x,y) = 0 + |y_i-z_i|, equal. Thus d(z,y) = d(z,x)+d(x,y) for any feasible y? Let's check for right move: For block i, z_i<c≤y_i, so |y_i-z_i| = (c-z_i)+(y_i-c) = |x_i-z_i|+|y_i-x_i|. For i<k, z_i≤z_k<c≤? y_i could be anything ≤c? Feasible y has y_i≤c for i<k. If y_i<z_i? Could lower optional left persons; then x_i=z_i, d(z,y)=|z_i-y_i|, d(z,x)+d(x,y)=0+|z_i-y_i| equal. If y_i>z_i but ≤c, x_i=z_i, equal. For i≥j, z_i≥c, feasible y_i≥c; x_i=z_i, equal. Thus d(z,y) = d(z,x)+d(x,y) for all feasible y! Wait if y_i for i<k could be >c? No, nondecreasing with a_k=c implies y_i≤c. So yes. For right move, x is the coordinatewise minimum? Actually x_i = max(z_i,c)? For i>k, x_i=max(z_i,c); for i<k, x_i=z_i. Feasible y must have y_i≥x_i for i>k and y_i≤? For i<k, y_i≤c but could be less than z_i; x_i=z_i. The equality d(z,y)=d(z,x)+d(x,y) holds because for each coordinate, if x_i between z_i and y_i? Need check i<k where z_i maybe > y_i? x_i=z_i, so |z_i-y_i| = 0 + |x_i-y_i|. If y_i>z_i, x_i=z_i, equal. For i>k, x_i=max(z_i,c). If z_i≥c, x_i=z_i, equal. If z_i<c, x_i=c, and y_i≥c, so c between. Thus yes. Therefore the minimal update x lies on a shortest path from z to any feasible y. Hence replacing y by x at task t and then moving x→y before next task costs d(z,x)+d(x,y) = d(z,y), no more than original d(z,y) (actually equal), and then continue. Wait original cost segment z→y plus y→next. New cost z→x + x→y + y→next = d(z,y)+d(y,next) if d(z,y)=d(z,x)+d(x,y). Equal. So we can insert x without increasing. But after task t, the configuration must be y (or x?) The task requires a_k=c; both x and y satisfy. If we choose x at task t, then to reach y before next task costs d(x,y). Total same. Thus there exists optimal where at task t we use x. By induction, greedy minimal update at each task is optimal. Great! This is a strong proof. So myopic greedy is not just heuristic; it's exact because the minimal projection lies on all geodesics to any feasible configuration. Nice.

This also means our update is forced in the sense of optimal path.

Full code:

```
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    ptr = 0
    N = data[ptr]; ptr += 1
    a = [data[ptr + i] - i for i in range(N)]
    ptr += N

    size = 1
    log = 0
    while size < N:
        size <<= 1
        log += 1

    sumv = [0] * (2 * size)
    maxv = [0] * (2 * size)
    lazy = [None] * (2 * size)
    length = [0] * (2 * size)

    NEG_INF = -10**30

    for i in range(size, 2 * size):
        length[i] = 1
    for i in range(size - 1, 0, -1):
        length[i] = length[i << 1] + length[(i << 1) | 1]

    for i, v in enumerate(a):
        sumv[size + i] = v
        maxv[size + i] = v
    for i in range(N, size):
        maxv[size + i] = NEG_INF
    for i in range(size - 1, 0, -1):
        lc = i << 1
        rc = lc | 1
        sumv[i] = sumv[lc] + sumv[rc]
        maxv[i] = maxv[lc] if maxv[lc] >= maxv[rc] else maxv[rc]

    def all_apply(k, val):
        sumv[k] = val * length[k]
        maxv[k] = val
        if k < size:
            lazy[k] = val

    def push(k):
        v = lazy[k]
        if v is not None:
            lc = k << 1
            rc = lc | 1
            sumv[lc] = v * length[lc]
            maxv[lc] = v
            if lc < size:
                lazy[lc] = v
            sumv[rc] = v * length[rc]
            maxv[rc] = v
            if rc < size:
                lazy[rc] = v
            lazy[k] = None

    def pull(k):
        lc = k << 1
        rc = lc | 1
        sumv[k] = sumv[lc] + sumv[rc]
        maxv[k] = maxv[lc] if maxv[lc] >= maxv[rc] else maxv[rc]

    def range_apply_sum(l, r, val):
        if l >= r:
            return 0
        l += size
        r += size
        l0 = l
        r0 = r
        for i in range(log, 0, -1):
            if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
                push(l0 >> i)
            if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
                push((r0 - 1) >> i)
        old = 0
        while l < r:
            if l & 1:
                old += sumv[l]
                all_apply(l, val)
                l += 1
            if r & 1:
                r -= 1
                old += sumv[r]
                all_apply(r, val)
            l >>= 1
            r >>= 1
        l = l0
        r = r0
        for i in range(1, log + 1):
            if ((l >> i) << i) != l:
                pull(l >> i)
            if ((r >> i) << i) != r:
                pull((r - 1) >> i)
        return old

    def point_get(p):
        p += size
        for i in range(log, 0, -1):
            push(p >> i)
        return maxv[p]

    def find_first_ge(x):
        if maxv[1] < x:
            return N
        k = 1
        while k < size:
            push(k)
            lc = k << 1
            if maxv[lc] >= x:
                k = lc
            else:
                k = lc | 1
        idx = k - size
        return idx if idx < N else N

    Q = data[ptr]; ptr += 1
    ans = 0
    for _ in range(Q):
        T = data[ptr]
        G = data[ptr + 1]
        ptr += 2
        k = T - 1
        c = G - k
        cur = point_get(k)
        if cur == c:
            continue
        if cur < c:
            j = find_first_ge(c)
            R = j - 1
            old = range_apply_sum(k, R + 1, c)
            ans += (R - k + 1) * c - old
        else:
            j = find_first_ge(c + 1)
            L = j
            old = range_apply_sum(L, k + 1, c)
            ans += old - (k - L + 1) * c

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

### Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "lazy" if a node is assigned a value and then later `pull` on it without push; we argued no. ### Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "find_first_ge(c+1)" in left if c+1 less than all a; returns 0, L=0. Good.

### Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=1" and `find_first_ge` if maxv[1] < x returns N=1; if maxv[1]>=x, while false, idx=0. Good.

### Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "size=1" and `length` array length 2; build for i in range(size,2
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    ptr = 0

    N = data[ptr]
    ptr += 1

    a = [data[ptr + i] - i for i in range(N)]
    ptr += N

    size = 1
    log = 0
    while size < N:
        size <<= 1
        log += 1

    sumv = [0] * (2 * size)
    maxv = [0] * (2 * size)
    lazy = [None] * (2 * size)
    length = [0] * (2 * size)

    NEG_INF = -10**30

    for i in range(size, 2 * size):
        length[i] = 1
    for i in range(size - 1, 0, -1):
        length[i] = length[i << 1] + length[(i << 1) | 1]

    for i, v in enumerate(a):
        sumv[size + i] = v
        maxv[size + i] = v
    for i in range(N, size):
        maxv[size + i] = NEG_INF

    for i in range(size - 1, 0, -1):
        lc = i << 1
        rc = lc | 1
        sumv[i] = sumv[lc] + sumv[rc]
        maxv[i] = maxv[lc] if maxv[lc] >= maxv[rc] else maxv[rc]

    def all_apply(k, val):
        sumv[k] = val * length[k]
        maxv[k] = val
        if k < size:
            lazy[k] = val

    def push(k):
        v = lazy[k]
        if v is not None:
            lc = k << 1
            rc = lc | 1
            sumv[lc] = v * length[lc]
            maxv[lc] = v
            if lc < size:
                lazy[lc] = v

            sumv[rc] = v * length[rc]
            maxv[rc] = v
            if rc < size:
                lazy[rc] = v

            lazy[k] = None

    def pull(k):
        lc = k << 1
        rc = lc | 1
        sumv[k] = sumv[lc] + sumv[rc]
        maxv[k] = maxv[lc] if maxv[lc] >= maxv[rc] else maxv[rc]

    def range_apply_sum(l, r, val):
        if l >= r:
            return 0

        l += size
        r += size
        l0 = l
        r0 = r

        for i in range(log, 0, -1):
            if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
                push(l0 >> i)
            if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
                push((r0 - 1) >> i)

        old = 0
        while l < r:
            if l & 1:
                old += sumv[l]
                all_apply(l, val)
                l += 1
            if r & 1:
                r -= 1
                old += sumv[r]
                all_apply(r, val)
            l >>= 1
            r >>= 1

        l = l0
        r = r0
        for i in range(1, log + 1):
            if ((l >> i) << i) != l:
                pull(l >> i)
            if ((r >> i) << i) != r:
                pull((r - 1) >> i)

        return old

    def point_get(p):
        p += size
        for i in range(log, 0, -1):
            push(p >> i)
        return maxv[p]

    def find_first_ge(x):
        if maxv[1] < x:
            return N

        k = 1
        while k < size:
            push(k)
            lc = k << 1
            if maxv[lc] >= x:
                k = lc
            else:
                k = lc | 1

        idx = k - size
        return idx if idx < N else N

    Q = data[ptr]
    ptr += 1

    ans = 0

    for _ in range(Q):
        T = data[ptr]
        G = data[ptr + 1]
        ptr += 2

        k = T - 1
        c = G - k

        cur = point_get(k)

        if cur == c:
            continue

        if cur < c:
            j = find_first_ge(c)
            R = j - 1
            old = range_apply_sum(k, R + 1, c)
            ans += (R - k + 1) * c - old
        else:
            j = find_first_ge(c + 1)
            L = j
            old = range_apply_sum(L, k + 1, c)
            ans += old - (k - L + 1) * c

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
